NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
Fern Benally • Alberto L. Peshlakai • Jason E. Whiting • Daryl Seymore • Dawnafe Whitesinger

NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING AND AGENDA
Tuesday, June 23, 2026
NAVAJO COUNTY GOVERNMENTAL COMPLEX
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS' CHAMBERS
100 EAST CODE TALKERS DRIVE
HOLBROOK, AZ 86025
One or more supervisors may attend telephonically
Pursuant to A.R.S. §38-431.02(H), the public body will have physical access to the meeting place one (1) hour prior to the start of
the meeting. The Board may vote to meet in a closed executive session to discuss certain matters and for legal advice on any item
as indicated on the following agenda, pursuant to A.R.S. § 38-431.03(A)(3). Items on the agenda may be considered out of order at
the Chairperson's discretion. A copy of the agenda background material provided to the Board, except items to be considered in the
executive session, is available for public inspection at the Navajo County website, www.navajocountyaz.gov 
WATCH THE MEETING LIVE AT: www.navajocountyaz.gov/660
All public comments will need to be made in person or in writing. Written comments will be received by the Clerk of the Board's
office twenty-four (24) hours prior to the Board meeting, at leah.thomas@navajocountyaz.gov. 
Individuals seeking to address the Board during the Call to the Public or when an agenda item is called must fill out a
“Request to Speak” form and provide it to the Clerk of the Board of Supervisors before the meeting is called to order. Late
submissions will not be accepted. The form must indicate whether the item the person wishes to speak on is on the agenda.
 Members of the public present to speak to the Board on an agenda item must so indicate, and are asked to refrain from speaking
during the general Call to the Public.  Persons present to address agenda items will be called on during a public comment period by
the Board Chairman after the relevant agenda item is called. Individuals addressing the Board during the general Call to the Public
are limited to three minutes.  Individuals addressing the Board when an item is called are also limited to three minutes, however,
additional time may be allotted by the Chairman to address any specific questions asked by members of the Board.
Persons with disabilities who need accommodation to attend or participate in the meeting may contact the Clerk of the Board’s
office at least forty-eight (48) hours prior to the meeting, so accommodation can be arranged.
“NOTICE TO PARENTS AND LEGAL GUARDIANS: Parents and legal guardians have the right to consent before Navajo County makes a video or voice recording
of a minor child, pursuant to A.R.S. § 1-602(A)(9). The Navajo County Board of Supervisors' regular meetings are recorded and may be viewed on Navajo County’s
website and social media pages. If you permit your child to attend/participate in a televised Navajo County Board of Supervisors meeting, a recording will be made, and
your child’s picture may be posted on Navajo County’s social media pages. You may exercise your right not to consent by not allowing your child to attend/participate
in the meeting.”

NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS’ MINUTES
Tuesday, June 9, 2026
Present: Fern Benally, Supervisor District I; Alberto Peshlakai, Supervisor
District II (appearing virtual); Jason Whiting, Supervisor District III;
Daryl Seymore, Chairman; Dawnafe Whitesinger, Vice-Chair 
Staff
Attendance: 
Bryan Layton, County Manager, Brad Carlyon, County Attorney,
Leah Thomas, Clerk of the Board 
9:00 a.m
. Call To Order:Invocation and Pledge of Allegiance was led by Supervisor
Whiting. 
Chairman Seymore asked those in attendance to remember to have decorum while
speakers are presenting. He then thanked Alicia Deets for arranging the National
Anthem performers. 
The National Anthem was performed by Lacey Deets.
After the prayer and the National Anthem, Chairman Seymore stated that item 7c.
would be presented before items 7a. and 7b.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger arrived at 9:05 a.m. 
CALL TO THE PUBLIC: 
Michael Pitts addressed the Board and requested that the County impose an immediate
moratorium on all new and pending utility-scale wind, solar, battery storage, and
data-center projects under A.R.S. 11-833. He stated that the purpose of the moratorium
would be to allow time to develop stronger and more protective regulations related to
setbacks, structure heights, noise limits, visual impacts, decommissioning, road impacts,
fire protection, environmental effects, and protections for neighboring landowners. Mr.
Pitts expressed concern that recent Board actions, including Resolution 01-2025 and
Resolution 17-2025, have weakened protections. He urged the Board to pause
approvals, rescind those resolutions, restore prior standards, and strengthen them as
needed before additional projects proceed. He emphasized that once such infrastructure
is constructed, the impacts are long-term, and residents deserve assurance that
regulations prioritize local communities.
Liz Olmstead from Show Low, addressed the Board and provided examples of other
Arizona counties that have imposed moratoriums, bans, or restrictive zoning on
large-scale renewable energy and data-center projects without being overturned in
court. She referenced actions in Yavapai, Mohave, Cochise, Pima, and the City of
Tucson involving restrictions on large-scale wind and solar development, data-center
regulations, and potable-water use limitations. Ms. Olmstead urged the Board to stop
encouraging such developers to locate projects in the region, stating that
these developments negatively impact the local environment. She asked the Board to
support the priorities of local constituents and consider the concerns being raised by
residents.
Donna Wastchak addressed the Board and encouraged the County leadership to
engage with the community to work together to explore alternatives to large-scale wind,
solar, and data-center development. She emphasized the potential for ecotourism,
dark-sky opportunities, and other economic options that highlight the area’s natural
beauty and benefit a broader portion of the community. Ms. Wastchak expressed
concerns about water use associated with proposed developments and noted the
challenges faced by local cattle ranchers. She referenced A.R.S. 11 1101, stating her
1 of 16 
Draft

<!-- page 8 -->
belief that it provides the Board with authority to ensure development aligns with the
County’s comprehensive plan and protects community interests. She encouraged the
Board to consider a round-table discussion to evaluate alternative economic pathways
and noted that recent proposals appear to benefit only a limited number of landowners. 
 
COUNTY BUSINESS UPDATE:
Bryan Layton, County Manager, provided an operational update and highlighted the
monthly “County Snapshot” posted on the County’s website and social media. He
recognized significant accomplishments within Public Works, including the installation or
replacement of 93 road signs, the collection of over 7,000 tires for recycling, and
maintenance or grading of more than 500 miles of roadway during April. Mr. Layton also
acknowledged the work of Planning and Development Services, noting improved
efficiency resulting from the Board’s prior investment in digitizing department processes.
He recognized staff leadership in implementing the new system and reported that in
April, the department closed 28 code-compliance cases and opened 31, a level of
progress not previously seen. He thanked staff for their continued efforts and reminded
the Board that the monthly update is available for public viewing.
Supervisor Whiting reported that over the past several weeks he has spoken with
residents and community leaders regarding issues such as water, agriculture,
renewable-energy development, economic opportunity, and the future of rural
communities. He noted that many residents have asked how their concerns can be
communicated to the agencies and organizations involved in decisions affecting Navajo
County. Supervisor Whiting stated that he has been sharing those concerns with the
appropriate agencies and organizations to ensure local perspectives are understood. He
emphasized that many of these issues involve multiple levels of government and
decision-makers and encouraged residents to engage directly with the relevant agencies
as well. He reaffirmed his commitment to listening carefully, communicating honestly,
exercising the authority of his office responsibly, and making decisions based on the
law, the facts, and what he believes is in the long-term best interest of Navajo County.
Supervisor Benally reported on recent activities within District 1. She informed the
Board that she has been working to fill an opening on the Planning and Zoning
Commission and has reached out to the Winslow City Council. She attended the County
5K Run and Walk on May 27 in Kayenta. On June 1, she attended the Navajo Nation’s
Memorial Day event in Window Rock, where she greeted the Navajo Nation
President and Council Speaker and participated in honoring veterans. Supervisor
Benally also provided updates on transportation discussions involving U.S. Highway
160, State Route 98, and U.S. Highway 163, noting safety topics such as crosswalks,
pedestrian lighting, and speed limits. She attended a Silver Jackets meeting in Chinle
with the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers regarding flooding concerns affecting the areas
of Bird Springs and Tolani Lake. She also reported that she attended a community
healthcare event in Shonto Canyon, where several County and Navajo Nation entities
participated in sharing resources. She reported speaking with parents of students from
Baker Middle School who recently competed in an international robotics event in Long
Beach, where the team placed in the thirties.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger thanked the members of the public in attendance and
emphasized the importance of community participation. She reported ongoing
partnership-building efforts with the White Mountain Apache Tribe following the election
of new tribal leadership. She noted a recent breakfast meeting with the Tribe’s
                                                                    2 of 16 
Draft

<!-- page 9 -->
Chairman, the Mayor of Pinetop-Lakeside, and the Mayor of Show Low, highlighting the
value of building strong relationships among neighboring governments. Vice
Chairwoman Whitesinger also provided updates on continued work between the
County’s Transportation Department and the White Mountain Apache Tribe regarding
roadway needs and small infrastructure projects. She shared information about the
State Route 260 Lion Springs Improvement Project, noting its $76.2 million cost,
completion of the final design phase by ADOT, and anticipated construction beginning in
June 2026. She recognized the extensive public advocacy that helped keep the project
in the state’s five-year plan and acknowledged its importance to public safety and
regional commerce.
Supervisor Peshlakai reported that he has been productive since the last Board
meeting. On May 27, he toured a long-planned C-store construction project in Indian
Wells, with the Navajo Nation President, noting its economic significance for the
community. He participated in the Winslow Indian Health Care Center’s “Just Move It”
5K walk/run series on May 28, stating that the series of events will be held weekly
through late July, providing opportunities for community engagement. On June 4, he
served as master of ceremonies at a community event in Bird Springs, where he also
provided county and district updates and spoke on health, wellness, and available
County services. Supervisor Peshlakai reported involvement in the Holbrook Route 66
Gunslinger and Car Show, coordinating a 5K run on the County trail with 11
participants. He continues to participate in meetings of the Northern Arizona Council of
Governments Economic Development Committee, as well as graduation-related
speaking engagements. He concluded by noting ongoing collaboration with tribal
partners and chapter communities via in-person and virtual meetings.
Chairman Seymore reported meeting with constituents in the Linden area regarding
flooding impacts from a significant storm event last year. He noted efforts to obtain bids
for the removal of several large trees at the Pinedale Cemetery, emphasizing the need
for careful work to avoid damage to headstones. He also met with the local fire chief to
discuss a proposed $25 million bond and the importance of maintaining fire-district
services for community safety. Chairman Seymore reminded the public that elections
are approaching and encouraged residents to review election materials and participate.
He highlighted an upcoming community project in Heber-Overgaard, where a 155-foot
flagpole will be erected on July 3 to display a large American flag for the 4th of July
holiday. He concluded with a personal reflection on the unpredictability of life,
referencing a recent accident involving his grandson, and encouraged the community to
value life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness.

PLANNING & DEVELOPMENT SERVICES: 
a. Presentation on A Home of My Own program
The County Website, Planning and Development Services page was shown.
Mischa Larish presented the Home of My Own program, created to support
workforce housing in Navajo County. He explained that the County purchased
six fully designed single-family home plans, which are now available at no cost
for anyone building within the County’s jurisdiction. He said that the State
mandate applies only to municipalities, but the County initiated the program too,
due to local housing needs. Mr. Larish noted that the plans, which range from
576 to 1,613 square feet, include multiple elevation options and garage or
carport choices. All plans have been reviewed and stamped by the County, and
truss engineering has been started by Ballard Truss. This results in a minimum
savings of $2,275 for applicants, as architectural plan and review fees are
eliminated. Permits can be issued quickly once an applicant submits a simple
option sheet. He added that the homes may also be used as accessory dwelling
units under the County’s ADU ordinance. The program aligns with similar efforts
statewide and was originally initiated through legislative work supported by
Senator Sinema. Architect Mark Rogers provided the designs, allowing the
County to make them freely available for public use.
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger asked for clarification on where the program
can be found on the website. 
Mr. Larish provided the information. 
Chairman Seymore asked if someone living in a municipality could use the
                                                                    4 of 16 
Draft

<!-- page 11 -->
plans.
Mr. Larish explained that the architect is currently working with every
municipality in Navajo County.  He pointed out that the municipality of
Snowflake may not have their program ready by the July 1st deadline, but that
Navajo County Planning and Development Services has agreed to let them
utilize the County’s plans until their program is established.
 
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS:
a. PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and either a recommendation to the
Department of Liquor Licenses & Control for approval or disapproval of the
pending Liquor License Application from Theodore Van Gaines III (Agent) for
Love’s Travel Stop #278 located at 4703 Main Street, Joseph City, AZ 86032,
for a 010 Beer and Wine Store liquor license.
Amanda Silverhorn reported that a Series 010 Beer and Wine Store License
application had been received from Theodore Von Gaines, agent for Love’s
Travel Stop #278 located at 4703 Main Street in Joseph City. She stated that the
Sheriff’s Office, Treasurer’s Office, Health Department, and Planning and
Development had all responded that the application met their respective
requirements and that the required 21-day posting period had also been
completed. Ms. Silverhorn explained that staff conducted research into the
history of the location. The truck stop was constructed in the 1997-1998
timeframe. Staff reviewed all Board of Supervisors agendas and minutes dating
back to 1995 and found no record of any previous liquor license applications for
this location.
She further stated that staff contacted the Arizona Department of Liquor
Licenses and Control, which confirmed that it has no record of any prior
applications for Love’s in Joseph City other than the current pending application.
The Department noted that records for denied or surrendered licenses are
retained for only ten years. Staff recommended that the Board consider the
information presented when making its recommendation to the Arizona
Department of Liquor Licenses and Control.
Chairman Seymore opened the meeting for public comments. 
Karilyn Bushman provided a copy of a petition to the Clerk and addressed the
Board. She stated that, upon further research, it came to her attention that
Love’s #278 did not submit a completed liquor-license application in past years.
She noted that the objections raised by the community in 1997-1998, and again
at a later date, did not proceed because the applications were never completed.
Ms. Bushman stated that there had been visible public notice postings during
prior attempts, and the community organized opposition to the proposed liquor
license on those occasions. She further shared that Jean Varney has
researched safety concerns related to the addition of a liquor license in the
community and would be addressing those findings to the Board.  Ms. Bushman
noted that a representative from the Joseph City School District was unable to
attend, but that the district has concerns as the campus remains open, with the
baseball field located less than a mile from the Love’s location and no sidewalks
in the area. She explained that the school hosts summer sports programs and
camps, including football and baseball, and one of the reasons outside groups
choose this location is the absence of liquor sales in the community, which
provides greater control and safety for visiting students. Ms. Bushman stated
that multiple community members support maintaining a community without
liquor sales and expressed opposition to the proposed liquor license. She also
                                                                    5 of 16 
Draft

<!-- page 12 -->
referenced the petition provided to the Board and stated that it includes concerns
regarding the lack of 24/7 law enforcement presence in the area. Ms. Bushman
concluded by urging the Board not to approve a liquor license in the
community.
Jean Varney provided printed information to the Board. 
Ms. Varney addressed the Board and stated she has lived in Joseph City for 10
years and had been a visitor to the community for 45 years prior to living there.
She spoke regarding safety concerns related to the proposed liquor license. Ms.
Varney reported that an average of approximately 3,225 vehicles use Exit 277,
where Love’s is located, each day, totaling roughly 1.18 million vehicles
annually. She noted that this represents many individuals who would have
access to alcohol if it were made available at that location. She stated she
initially believed the application was for a beer and wine bar, and while alcohol
cannot be consumed inside the store, the parking lot is not considered part of
the premises, creating an opportunity for consumption despite it being illegal.
She explained that nearly half of the vehicles are semi-trucks, with 58 parking
spaces at Love’s that are fully occupied each night. Due to limited parking, many
trucks park along roadsides, on-ramps, and off ramps as well. She expressed
concern that travelers purchasing closed containers would be more likely to
consume alcohol immediately rather than waiting hours to reach another
destination. Ms. Varney stated she reviewed the Painted Desert Tribune’s
archives for Sheriff’s Office reports from May 2011 through June 3, 2026, and
identified 15 incidents directly related to alcohol, including DUIs, open container
violations, and underage drinking. She added that many other incidents likely
involved alcohol as a contributing factor. She further identified 21 violent crime
incidents reported during the same period. 
Ms. Varney allotted speaking time concluded.
Dan Bushman agreed to let Ms. Varney use some of his allotted time.
Ms. Varney referenced a Sheriff’s Office report for emergency and urgent
priority calls in 2025, noting that 168 calls were recorded, with response times
ranging from approximately 12 to 20 minutes. She stated that research shows
legalizing on-premises alcohol sales in previously dry or restricted rural areas
leads to a 10% to 25% increase in violent crime, with a single liquor license
typically resulting in an additional 3.6 to 5 violent crimes per year. She
expressed concern that this would increase demands on the Sheriff’s Office and
heighten risk for residents.
Ms. Varney directed the Board to photos in the materials provided showing the
proximity of the store to the community and the school, noting the absence of
sidewalks and the frequency of pedestrian traffic. She stated that increased
traffic and the availability of alcohol would create greater opportunity for
accidents and unsafe situations along Main Street. She concluded by stating
that the community would prefer not to have an alcohol license at all.
Dan Bushman addressed the Board and thanked them for the opportunity to
speak. Mr. Bushman noted that he works for the school district and is heavily
involved in his church. He reported that during his 10 years with the school
district, and through his involvement in school sports, there have been multiple
occasions where school staff have had to escort intoxicated individuals off
school property. He commended the County Sheriff’s Office for its increased
presence in recent years and noted their use of school facilities for training
exercises. However, he stated he is concerned about response times.  Mr.
Bushman further stated that law enforcement has also been needed to remove
                                                                    6 of 16

intoxicated individuals from other public spaces, including church buildings. He
expressed concern about the pattern of intoxication related to these incidents
and stated that, in his view, there is a clear correlation. He concluded by
respectfully requesting that the Board deny the liquor license in Joseph City for
the safety of the community’s children.
Dillard Atwell addressed the Board and stated that he is the General Manager of
the Love’s Travel Center in Joseph City and was speaking on behalf of the

restricted sales. He affirmed that staff are trained not to sell to individuals who
appear intoxicated. He also noted that a nearby location, Jackrabbit, located four
to five miles away, already sells alcohol, and drivers can easily stop there as
well. Mr. Atwell stated that, based on his understanding, the store is more than
300 yards from any school activity area and therefore compliant with the
distance requirements under Arizona law. He acknowledged concerns about

contribute to issues involving individuals who may already be bringing alcohol
from elsewhere. He concluded by stating that this represents Love’s position on
the liquor license request.
Andrew Bushman addressed the Board and stated that, while he believes
Love’s would comply with all laws and requirements regarding a liquor license,
his concerns are directed at alcohol itself rather than at the business. He
acknowledged that Jackrabbit, located approximately five miles away, already
sells alcohol, but noted that the distance involved in traveling to and from that
location creates a meaningful difference for residents compared to alcohol being
available in their community. Mr. Bushman stated that he has no personal
issues with individuals who choose to drink and noted that many of his friends do

tenants of that property asked whether a fence could be installed if the license is
approved, due to concerns that individuals could enter the unfenced backyard to
seek shade or rest if intoxicated. Mr. Bushman stated that while he believes
businesses have the right to operate as they choose, he is concerned about the
additional burden that alcohol sales may place on Joseph City residents. He
noted that the community has historically been dry, dating back before
statehood, and has never faced the impacts of local alcohol sales. He also

referenced concerns about property values and the safety of his renters. He

Chairman Seymore closed the Public Hearing.
Chairman Seymore explained that the Board does not approve or deny liquor
licenses but makes a recommendation for approval or denial to Arizona
Department of Liquor Licenses and Control.  He said that the Board’s
responsibility is to look at what is presented and make their recommendation,
stating that he is appreciative of the research that has been done and the
presentations given. 
Supervisor Whiting asked Mr. Carlyon if the Board’s responsibility was to
evaluate whether the pending application meets the law.
Supervisor Peshlakai was offline at 10:16 a.m.
Supervisor Peshlakai was online at 10:19 a.m.
Brad Carlyon advised the Board that their role in the liquor-license process is to
evaluate the potential impact on the community and make a recommendation to
the State Liquor Board, which is the actual decision-making body. He stated that
public opposition alone typically carries limited weight with the Liquor Board;
however, objective facts demonstrating an adverse impact on the community
may influence the Board’s determination. Mr. Carlyon stated that he was
impressed with the members of the public at this hearing and at the prior hearing
held two weeks earlier, noting that they presented objective data the Board
could rely upon should it choose to recommend denial. He referred to a past
example involving alcohol sales in the Sanders/Chambers area, where the
community compiled data related to law-enforcement issues such as public
intoxication and assaults, which helped support the denial of a license transfer.
He explained that the Board may elevate community concerns by submitting the
information received, including the petition provided, law-enforcement records
referenced by one of the speakers, and any data gathered by the Sheriff’s
Office, to the State Liquor Board along with its recommendation. He stated that if
the Board’s majority believes a recommendation of denial is appropriate, it would
be beneficial to include an analysis of the objective data to emphasize why the
Board has those concerns.
Supervisor Whiting thanked Mr. Carlyon for the clarification and stated that it is
important for the community to understand who ultimately makes the decision
regarding the liquor-license application. He noted that regardless of the outcome
of the Board’s discussion today, the community’s voice will need to continue
before the State Liquor Board. He stated that he, personally, can assist in
elevating those concerns to the appropriate body. 
Vice Chairwoman Whitesinger stated that if the Board were to recommend
denial of the liquor-license application to the Arizona State Liquor Board, one
argument the Board could elevate is the community’s historical status as a dry
town. She remarked that Joseph City may be one of the only dry communities in
Arizona, which she described as historically significant. Vice Chairwoman
Whitesinger stated that protecting that long-standing historical characteristic,
spanning approximately 150 years, may be an important consideration.
She emphasized that community identity and the values of the people who live
there should be recognized as part of the discussion if the Board chooses to
move in that direction.
Supervisor Peshlakai thanked the community members who submitted
petitions and provided public comment regarding the liquor-license application.
He stated that he appreciated the advocacy efforts demonstrated by residents.

<!-- page 15 -->
He then outlined additional concerns he believed were not previously
addressed. Supervisor Peshlakai noted that Joseph City is connected to the
southern portion of the Navajo Nation by a 13-mile county road (Territorial
Road). He explained that the Navajo Nation prohibits alcohol sales, except for
limited allowances at casinos located on fee lands. He stated that many tribal
communities experience ongoing issues with alcohol-related harm, including
DUI incidents and traffic accidents. He expressed concern that, due to the lack
of law-enforcement presence on the Territorial Road, the availability of alcohol at
the proposed location could create an unintended gateway for individuals
transporting alcohol into the Navajo Nation, including potential bootlegging
operations. He contrasted this with the high visibility of law enforcement along
State Route 77, Navajo Route 6, and other monitored highways. Supervisor
Peshlakai further noted that the proposed liquor outlet is located fewer than 15
miles from the Navajo Nation boundary, and that communities within that
distance have long-faced disproportionate alcohol-related impacts. He stated
that additional data, including tribal statistics, should be considered alongside
information provided by the Sheriff’s Office and the Arizona Department of
Public Safety. He reiterated his respect for the concerns raised by Joseph City
residents and encouraged them to utilize their right under state statute to file a
formal protest with the Arizona Department of Liquor Licenses and Control. He
concluded by thanking the community for its participation and information.
Supervisor Benally stated that, after reviewing the photos, she noted the
absence of sidewalks in the area, which means pedestrians must walk along the
roadway. She commented that the proposed location appears to be very close
to the school and expressed concern about public safety. She further noted that
law enforcement staffing is limited, particularly in reservation communities, and
agreed with  Supervisor Peshlakai’s concerns regarding potential impacts on
Navajo Nation residents. She stated that allowing alcohol sales at the proposed
location could create an additional avenue for individuals from the reservation,
where alcohol is prohibited, to obtain liquor. Supervisor Benally stated that
liquor sales are generally pursued for profit and revenue but emphasized that if
the local community does not want alcohol sold among them, it may not be
appropriate to impose it upon them. She explained that while she has supported
liquor-license approvals in the past, this was not because she favors
widespread issuance, but because those proposals were for businesses
managing their own operations. In this case, however, she stated that
community members do not want alcohol in their area and should not be placed
in a situation that increases unsafe conditions or crime. She expressed concern
that some individuals may consume alcohol immediately upon exiting the store
and entering their vehicles, despite laws prohibiting such behavior. She added
that the absence of sidewalks further compounds safety risks, particularly for
pedestrians. She concluded by stating that, for these reasons, she does not
believe approval of the license would be a good idea.
Supervisor Whiting made a motion to decline the recommendation for
approval of the liquor license application and directed staff to provide the
documentation presented by the Joseph City community members to the
Arizona Department of Liquor Licenses and Control. The motion further directed
staff to include information regarding the historical nature of Joseph City as a dry
community when submitting the materials to the Department and to provide Ms.
Bushman with the contact information for the liquor board, motion seconded

ADMINISTRATIVE SERVICES: 
a. Consideration and adoption of the proposed Tentative Budget for Fiscal Year
2026-27 for Navajo County.
After item 7c., items 7a. and 7b. were presented
A presentation was shown. 
Jayson Vowell presented the Tentative Navajo County Budget for Fiscal Year
2026-27. He stated that significant work had been completed by staff, elected
officials, and department directors through numerous meetings to review
departmental needs and evaluate how to support operations from a fiscal
perspective. He said this collaborative process resulted in the document before
the Board.
Mr. Vowell highlighted the County’s budget priorities, including maintaining
adequate financial reserves, forecasting healthy revenue streams, responsible
stewardship of taxpayer resources, and presenting a structurally balanced
budget where recurring revenues meet or exceed recurring expenditures. He
noted that the Tentative Budget recommends no increase to primary or
secondary property-tax rates. He also described efforts to align financial
resources with operational needs across departments and to support
investments in employees, infrastructure, and equipment. Mr. Vowell outlined
several proposed capital improvements and investments, including roadway and
bridge work, vehicle and equipment purchases, building upgrades, AI redaction
software, vehicle-tracking software, and continued IT modernization efforts such
as cloud migration and ADA-compliant website updates. He detailed new
recommended positions for FY 27, including part-time positions in the Justice
Courts, positions within the County Attorney’s Office and the Public Fiduciary,
three and a half grant-funded positions in the Health District, and one position in
the Benefits Trust Fund. He also outlined recommended employee pay
adjustments, including a 3% cost-of-living adjustment, 2% performance pay,
various market adjustments in specific job titles to stay competitive, and
reinstatement of a $500 one-time payment in November. Mr. Vowell reviewed
the summary of the County’s major fund types, noting total prior-year
appropriations and the proposed FY 27 total budget of approximately $246.2
million. He highlighted property-tax components, secondary district levies, other
key revenue sources, and the County’s maintained financial-stability reserve of
approximately $11.3 million. He presented historical general-fund cash and
fund-balance trends, noting improved financial health since FY 21 and increased
alignment between cash and fund-balance levels. He also reviewed major
general-fund revenue sources, including state-shared sales tax, county sales
tax, and property tax, and discussed expenditure allocations by function such as
public safety, criminal justice, mandated services, countywide services,
administration, and capital projects. Mr. Vowell also reviewed budgets for the
Health District, Flood Control District, Library District, Jail District, and Public
Works, highlighting each fund’s revenue sources, expenditures, major projects,
and grant activity. He noted that the Jail District continues to benefit from
designated sales tax revenues and transfers required by statute, and he
described expenditure categories such as personnel, medical services,
supplies, and contingency. He reviewed the County’s outstanding debt,
                                                                    10 of 16

including the pension-obligation bonds issued in 2022 and refinancing bonds
related to the jail upgrade and Public Works building. He explained that the
pension-bond structure is projected to save the County approximately $11
million through FY 2038 compared to paying pension obligations entirely
through payroll rates. Mr. Vowell then outlined major capital-project investments,
including the Winslow Levee Project with a budget of approximately $31.1
million, fuel-system and fuel-tank upgrades, and a security-modernization
project for County facilities and the jail. He concluded by noting that the public
hearing for the Adopted Budget and the Truth-in-Taxation hearing will take place
at the next Board meeting on Tuesday, June 23. He stated that he was available
for Board questions or comments.

c. Presentation on Economic Development in Navajo County and the use of REAL
AZ branding.
Item 7c. was presented before items 7a. and 7b. 
A presentation was shown. 
Bryan Layton presented an overview regarding the structure, authority, and
current operations of the County’s regional economic development partnership.
He explained that Arizona counties are authorized under A.R.S. 11 254 to use
public resources for economic development purposes, including sharing
information about local advantages such as natural resources, infrastructure,
and workforce. He stated that collaboration among regional entities helps
strengthen economic opportunities and attract businesses. Mr. Layton reviewed
the history of the regional partnership, explaining that past leaders recognized
the challenges of economic development when undertaken by a single entity.
Over time, the partnership evolved into a nonprofit organization known first as
the Real AZ. Corridor and later as Real AZ. He noted that while using an
external nonprofit was at times advantageous, the governing body ultimately
voted to dissolve the nonprofit. Following dissolution, participating entities,
including Navajo County, returned to a regional partnership model operated
within county government. He outlined that Navajo County has since handled all
financial transactions for economic development as part of its regular operations
and continued using the Real AZ branding due to its established recognition. He
stated that the Board had received questions regarding the former nonprofit and
the current economic development structure, prompting staff to complete an
internal assessment as directed by the Board. Mr. Layton explained that the
assessment identified three areas for clarification and improvement: First, the
regional partnership is operating without a formal intergovernmental agreement.
Staff will begin developing a formal agreement outlining roles, responsibilities,
and governance among participating entities. Second, some meeting practices
still reflect the former nonprofit model. Staff will discontinue those remaining
elements and fully transition to a regional partnership format. Third, the Real AZ
brand does not clearly differentiate the current partnership from the former
nonprofit. Branding will be updated to clarify that the partnership is now an
operational function of participating governments, not a separate legal entity. He
emphasized that the regional partnership is not a public body, is not a regulatory
authority, and has no independent legal status. Activities are carried out by
county staff in their official capacities. Other participating entities may choose
different staffing structures. Mr. Layton stated that the partnership supports
economic opportunities in manufacturing, transportation, agriculture, forestry,
tourism, healthcare, technology, traditional energy, and small business
development. He clarified that the economic development department does not
make land-use decisions, zoning determinations, permit approvals, or
development agreements, as those remain within the jurisdiction of the
appropriate local entity. He concluded by noting that the regional approach
supports job creation, workforce development, infrastructure investment,
                                                                    13 of 16 
Draft

<!-- page 20 -->
business recruitment and retention, site readiness work, and regional marketing
efforts, and that these outcomes would be more difficult for individual entities to
achieve independently. 
Vice Chair Whitesinger asked if the presentation would be made available to
the public. 
Mr. Layton confirmed that it would be made available to the public.
Chairman Seymore stated that he appreciated the members of the public who
requested a review of Real AZ. He noted that during the call to the public, the
Board could not engage in dialogue, so staff was directed to conduct the review.
He expressed appreciation for the staff’s work, the modifications made, and the
clarifications provided moving forward.  He then opened the meeting for public
comment on the agenda item.
Lisa Vincenti with Civic Integrity Alliance addressed the Board and stated that
the Real AZ Economic Development Group, formerly a nonprofit entity, was
formally dissolved on October 17, 2024. She asserted that under Arizona law,
once a nonprofit is dissolved, it no longer legally exists, and that continuing to
operate in its name, collect money, sign agreements, or use county resources
for its activities would be improper and illegal. Ms. Vincenti stated that, in her
view, such actions occurred following the dissolution. She alleged that five days
after the nonprofit dissolved, the Board approved the sale of the former
Snowflake paper mill site to a newly formed LLC, and that Supervisor Whiting,
who was also listed as director of Real AZ at the time, signed the deed. She
further stated that the LLC listed a nonprofit associated with the Worsley family
as its statutory agent and asserted connections between that family and other
projects promoted by county staff. She also stated that county staff signed a
nondisclosure agreement on behalf of the dissolved entity with a developer and
claimed that the Board had adopted resolutions weakening renewable-energy
regulations during the same period. Ms. Vincenti stated that various county
officials had mischaracterized the operational status of Real AZ to constituents
and asserted that these actions undermined transparency and public trust. She
requested that the Board take several actions: discontinue the use of Real AZ
branding in economic development, rescind Resolutions 1 2025 and 17 2025,
and consider employment-related action regarding a staff member. When she
began to address a topic outside the scope of the agenda item, the Chair
advised her to limit comments to the agenda item. She concluded by stating that
reversing certain decisions and leadership actions was necessary to restore
public confidence.
Belinda Quog addressed the Board and began by discussing renewable-energy
projects; however, the Chair reminded her to limit her remarks to the agenda
item concerning Real AZ. Ms. Quog then stated that a March 24, 2026, Real AZ
presentation listed ten investment accomplishments, eight of which she said
were unverified when she submitted public-records requests. She stated that the
County had no records supporting claims of new housing manufacturers,
large-scale residential developers, major job creation, new technology or
recreation industries, or dozens of new companies investing in economic
growth. She asserted that the only growth she observed was in member fees
associated with Real AZ activities. Ms. Quog further stated that she believed the
County had attempted to limit public access to meetings, referenced
nondisclosure agreements, and cited her concerns regarding transparency. She
referenced a recent public meeting, stating that it had been initiated by the
                                                                    14 of 16 
Draft

<!-- page 21 -->
County but not widely advertised, resulting in a strong public reaction. She
asserted that certain actions by the Board and County staff violated multiple
Arizona statutes and also claimed that eight other counties exercise authority to
restrict renewable-energy projects. Ms. Quog reiterated her request for a
moratorium on such projects. The Chair advised her that topics outside the
agenda item could not be considered during this portion of the meeting. Ms.
Quog concluded by stating that she believed there had been disregard for public
concerns related to renewable energy and REAL AZ and expressed that, in her

Chairman Seymore responded to public comments, stating that the Board had
paused approvals for approximately a year and a half while reviewing and
strengthening the County’s ordinance. He stated that no renewable-energy
projects or actions from Real AZ had been pushed to the Board during that time.
He addressed statements regarding “secret meetings,” stating that such
meetings had not occurred. He requested that anyone alleging such meetings
provide details. Chairman Seymore emphasized that the Board has worked to
share information openly. He stated that, although some individuals continued to
criticize the Board, the Board had already taken steps that the public had
requested, including implementing a pause while strengthening protections in
the renewable*-energy ordinance, and changing the process from a Special Use
Permit to a Development Agreement, which he said provides the County with
stronger oversight and enforcement tools. He also noted that the County
identified a mechanism to generate revenue from these projects to offset
associated costs. Chairman Seymore stated that recent updates to Real AZ
clarified its role as a legally compliant regional partnership and that staff had
made improvements consistent with public concerns. He thanked staff for their
work and noted that the County seeks to promote development and economic
stability while honoring property-rights laws. He reflected on the region’s long
history with energy projects, noting that past generations supported projects
such as the Cholla Power Plant for the economic benefits they brought. He
stated that change is ongoing and that working collaboratively with developers
allows the County to shape better outcomes. Chairman Seymore reaffirmed
that the Board has attempted to operate transparently, stating that staff has
increased outreach efforts and that he is available to discuss concerns with
residents. He concluded by thanking staff for their efforts and expressing
appreciation for partnerships that support economic development in Navajo
County.

Board of Supervisors Regular 3. g.        
Meeting Date: 06/23/2026  
Title: Fixing of Constable salaries for January 2027
Submitted By: Eric Scott, HR Director
Department: Administrative Services Division: Human Resources
Motion before the Board:
Approval for updating of Constable salaries for January 2027
Background:
A.R.S. 11-424.01, which covers salaries of constables, Item A states “At the regular June meeting of the
several boards of supervisors preceding a general election, the boards shall fix the salaries of the
constables to be elected for the four-year period commencing on the first day of the following January.”
In following the above-mentioned statute, it is the recommendation of staff to fix the constables salaries
at the amounts below:
Holbrook Precinct 1 – $27,000.00
Winslow Precinct 2 – $36,000.00
Snowflake Precinct 3 – $50,000.00
Kayenta Precinct 4 – $15,000.00
Show Low Precinct 5 – $55,000.00
Pinetop Precinct 6 – $55,000.00
These recommendations are based off the statute above, including the number of registered voters and
documents served in each precinct.

723410166
ORDER, CALL AND NOTICE OF SPECIAL BOND ELECTION
TO THE QUALIFIED ELECTORS OF TIMBER MESA FIRE AND MEDICAL DISTRICT OF NAVAJO COUNTY,  
ARIZONA (THE “DISTRICT”):
A special bond election will be held on November 3, 2026 (the “Election”).  The purpose of the special bond election 
is to permit the qualified electors of the District to vote on the question included in the following form of the Official  
Ballot.  
______________________________
SAMPLE BALLOT
______________________________
Shall the District Board of Timber Mesa Fire and Medical District of Navajo County,  
Arizona, be authorized to sell and issue general obligation bonds of Timber Mesa Fire and 
Medical District of Navajo County, Arizona, in the aggregate principal amount of not to  
exceed $25,000,000, to provide funds to construct, renovate, improve, equip and furnish  
fire  stations  and  other  District  facilities,  acquire  apparatus,  vehicles  and  equipment, 
liquidate  indebtedness  with  respect  to  the  foregoing,  and  pay  all  necessary  costs  in 
connection therewith; said general obligation bonds to bear interest at a rate not to exceed  
12% per annum and to mature over a period of not to exceed 20 years from the date of their 
issuance and may be sold at prices that include premiums not greater than permitted by  
law?
FOR THE BONDS 
AGAINST THE BONDS 
The aggregate principal amount of the bonds to be authorized at the Election shall be $25,000,000; the maximum  
rate of interest to be paid thereon shall be not more than 12% per annum; the minimum and maximum number of years  
bonds of any issue or series authorized at the Election may run from their date shall be 1 year and 20 years, respectively;  
the District currently has no general obligation debt outstanding and the statutory debt limit of the District is $26,218,713,  
being 6% of the net limited assessed property value of the taxable property in the District (which debt limit is based on a  
value provided by the assessor of Navajo County, Arizona for 2025/2026).
See the next page for general voting information provided by Navajo County, Arizona, which includes the date and 
hours of the Election.  The Election will be a polling place election.  A resident of the State of Arizona is qualified to register 
to vote if such person is a citizen of the United States, will be eighteen years of age or more on or before the date of the next 
regular general election, has not been convicted of treason or a felony, unless restored to civil rights, and has not been  
adjudicated an incapacitated person.  See Title 16, Arizona Revised Statutes for additional information regarding voter  
registration and qualifications of electors.

ELECTION PROCESS
This election is consolidated with the federal, state, and county's fall election cycle and will be a traditional, polling place  
(walk-in) election. The last day to register to vote in the November 3, 2026, General Election is October 5, 2026.
Any registered voters of the Timber Mesa Fire and Medical District  who wish to receive a ballot through the mail must  
contact Navajo County Voter Registration at (800) 668-3867 or (928) 524-4192 to request an “early ballot” or request being 
added to the Active Early Voting List. Early voting begins October 7, 2026. The last day to request an early ballot to be  
mailed is October 23, 2026. 
For this election, Timber Mesa Fire and Medical District voters may vote at
any of the following polling locations:
[To be provided by County]
Polling places will be open from 6:00 a.m. to 7:00 p.m. on November 3, 2026.
 
In Navajo County, voters can vote at any location nearest them, to see a full list of voting locations please visit. 
[https://www.navajocountyaz.gov/534/Polling-Places.]
**Navajo  County  is  pleased  to  announce  a  Ballot  Tracking  system.  Voters  will  be  able  to  sign  up  at  
[https://trackmyballot.azsos.gov/voter/] and get updates on where their ballot is via text message, email, or a phone call  
from the system. For more information, please call Navajo County Election at 928-524-4062.

Board of Supervisors Regular 3. j.        
Meeting Date: 06/23/2026  
Title: Stanley Consultants, Inc. - Professional Services Agreement for Shumway Road at Silver
Creek Bridge Replacement Project
Submitted For: William Flake, County Engineer 
Submitted By: Kathleen Outland, Procurement Officer
Department: Public Works
Motion before the Board:
Approval of a Professional Services Agreement with Stanley Consultants, Inc for Right-of-Way services
for the Shumway Road at Silver Creek Bridge Replacement Project
Background:
Navajo County Public Works is advancing the Shumway Road at Silver Creek Bridge Replacement
Project in District III. An Intergovernmental Agreement (IGA) with the Arizona Department of
Transportation (ADOT) for this project was approved by the Board of Supervisors on February 10, 2026.
Under that IGA, ADOT will administer the design development, advertisement, bidding, award, and
construction phases of the project.
As the design progresses, right-of-way (ROW) acquisitions are required to support the planned
realignment of a portion of Shumway Road and the replacement of the existing bridge. Stanley
Consultants, Inc., working under ADOT as the engineer for the project, has submitted a proposal to
Navajo County for ROW acquisition services. The scope of work includes preparing ROW plans and
legal descriptions, and performing all necessary title work, appraisals, negotiations, and acquisitions.
ADOT’s Right-of-Way Group will oversee the activities, while Navajo County will fund the ROW
component of the project directly, as it was not included in the awarded grant for the project. The total
lump-sum cost for these services, as proposed by Stanley Consultants and its subconsultants, is
$72,098.66.
Fiscal Impact
Fiscal Year: 2027 
Budgeted Y/N: Y 
Amount Requested: 72098.66

 CONTRACT FOR PROFESSIONAL SERVICES 
 
 
AGREEMENT made as of June 23, 2026, between Navajo County ("County") and 
Stanley Consultants, Inc. (“Consultant”). 
 
WHEREAS, County requires certain professional services for Shumway Road over 
Silver Creek Bridge Replacement Right-of-Way Services Project  (“Project”); and 
 
WHEREAS, Consultant represents that it has the necessary expertise to provide such 
services in accordance with the terms of this Agreement. 
 
NOW, THEREFORE, the parties agree as follows: 
 
1. Services.  Consultant shall perform the work described in the Scope of Work dated 
March 4, 2026, and those additional items set forth in the Consultant’s schedule and 
fee proposal dated  March 4, 2026, in a competent and professional manner to the 
satisfaction of County.  The Scope of Work and the Consultant’s schedule and fee 
proposal are attached hereto and by this reference incorporated herein.  If any 
incorporated term is inconsistent with the Agreement, this Agreement shall control.   
 
2. Consultant’s Expertise.  Consultant warrants that it has the ability, authority, 
capacity and professional expertise to perform this Agreement.  Consultant shall 
provide an adequate staff of experienced personnel, capable of and devoted to the 
successful accomplishment of the work to be performed hereunder.  Consultant shall 
assign specific individuals to key positions.  Once assigned to work under this 
Agreement, key personnel shall not be removed or replaced without County’s prior 
written approval.  
 
3. Independent Contractor.  Consultant acknowledges that it is an independent 
contractor.  Neither party is or shall purport to be an agent, employee, partner, joint 
venturer or associate of the other.  An employee or agent of one party shall not be 
deemed an employer or agent  of the other party for any purpose.  Taxes or Social 
Security contributions will not be withheld from any payment by County, and 
Consultant shall be solely responsible for such matters.

<!-- page 41 -->
Page -2- 
4. Subcontracts.  Consultant shall not enter into any subcontract with respect to any of 
the work to be performed hereunder without County’s prior written approval.  All 
subcontracts shall comply with applicable federal and state laws and regulations and 
shall impose on the subcontractor substantially the same obligations as are imposed 
on Consultant by this Agreement with respect to those matters covered by Sections 8, 
9, 10, 12, 15, 18, 21  and 22.  Consultant is responsible for full performance of this 
Agreement regardless of whether subcontractors are used. 
 
5. Time for Completion.  Consultant’s services will be provided in a manner consistent 
with the degree of car e and skill ordinarily exercised by members of the same 
profession currently practicing under similar circumstances.  Consultant’s services 
shall be performed as expeditiously as is consistent with professional skill and care 
and the orderly progress of the Project, and in accordance with an agreed upon 
schedule for the performance of the Services hereunder.  Time limits established by a 
schedule approved by County shall not, except for reasonable cause, be exceeded by 
County or Consultant.  The Schedule shall be adjusted, if necessary, as the Project 
proceeds.  The date for final performance shall be extended by the number of days 
that performance is delayed by governmental approval or review procedures or other 
causes beyond Consultant’s reasonable control, as jointly confirmed in writing by the 
parties’ representatives. 
 
6. Payment.  County shall pay to Consultant a not-to- exceed sum of seventy two 
thousand, ninety eight dollars and sixty six cents ($72,098.66) as payment in full for 
all services rendered by Consultant pursuant to this Agreement.  Payments shall be 
made within 30 days of County’s receipt of Consultant’s monthly invoices.  Each 
invoice shall detail the work performed during the billing period.  Invoice amounts in 
percent of Project cost shall not exceed the percentage of completion of the Project as 
approved by County.   
 
7. Defects in Work.  County may reject any work product that fails to meet customary 
professional standards or Project specifications.  Consultant agrees to promptly 
remedy all such deficiencies.  The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any 
controversy or claim through informal negotiation as set forth in Section 12.  No 
compensation shall be paid for any rejected work until such issues have been 
resolved. 
 
8. Insurance Requirements.   
 
A. Consultant shall maintain in effect, at all times during the term of this 
Agreement, insurance adequate to protect County and its agents, 
representatives, officers, officials and employees against such losses as set 
forth below.  Consultant shall provide County with a current Certificate of 
Insurance or a certified copy of the insurance policy naming County as an 
additional insured (except for Errors and Omissions / Professional Liability 
coverage). 
 
B. The following types and amounts of insurance are required as minimums:

<!-- page 42 -->
Page -3- 
  
Worker's Compensation    Statutory 
Professional Liability    $1,000,000 each occurrence 
           and annual aggregate 
Consultant’s Protective Bodily Injury  $1,000,000 each occurrence  
           and annual aggregate 
Consultant’s Protective Personal Property $1,000,000 each occurrence  
           and annual aggregate 
Automobile Bodily Injury and Property Damage $1,000,000 each occurrence 
       and annual aggregate 
Valuable Papers     $100,000 
 
Standard minimum deductibles are allowed.  Any deductibles are the 
responsibility of Consultant.  Consultant shall immediately inform County of 
any cancellation of insurance or any decrease in the amount of coverage at 
least 30 days before such action takes place.  A violation of this provision may 
be treated as a material breach by County.  Consultant shall notify County 
upon any termination of its regular professional liability coverage and shall 
obtain tail coverage for a minimum of five years from the termination date. 
 
C. Consultant acknowledges that the amounts of insurance coverage stated above 
shall not limit its liability under this Agreement. 
 
9. Indemnity.  To the fullest extent allowable by law, Consultant shall defend, 
indemnify and hold harmless County and its representatives, officers, officials and 
employees (the “Indemnitees”) from and against any and all claims, damages, losses 
and expenses (including but not limited to attorney fees, court costs, and the costs of 
appellate proceedings) relating to, arising out of or resulting from Consultant’s 
negligent acts, errors, mistakes or omissions in the performance of this Agreement. 
Consultant’s duty to defend, hold harmless and indemnify the Indemnitees shall arise 
in connection with any claim, damage, loss or expense that is attributable to bodily 
injury, sickness, disease, death or injury to, impairment or destruction of property 
including loss of use resulting therefrom, caused in whole or in part by any negligent 
act, error, mistake or omission in the performance of this Agreement (including those 
by any person for whose negligent acts, errors, mistakes or omissions Consultant may 
be liable) to the extent of Consultant’s negligence or fault.  The amount and type of 
insurance coverage requirements set forth above shall not limit the scope of the 
indemnity in this paragraph. 
 
10. Records.  Consultant shall retain, and shall require each subcontractor to retain, all 
books, accounts, reports, files and other records relating to this Agreement for a 
period of five years after completion of the work.  All such documents shall be 
subject to inspection and audit by County upon reasonable notice during normal 
business hours.  A legible copy of any or all such documents shall be produced by 
Consultant at the request and expense of County. 
 
11. Non-Assignment.  Consultant shall not assign any right or interest in this Agreement

<!-- page 43 -->
Page -4- 
without County’s prior written approval, nor shall Consultant delegate any duty 
hereunder without County’s prior written approval.   
 
12. Negotiation of Disputes.  The parties shall make a good faith effort to resolve any 
claim or controversy or claim through informal negotiation.  Notice of any claim or 
controversy shall be provided in writing, with supporting documentation, to the 
recipient designated in Section 24.  The recipient shall have seven calendar days to 
prepare and deliver a written response.  If the parties fail to resolve the disputes 
within a reasonable period of not less than ten days, either party may pursue available 
legal remedies. 
 
13. Suspension and Termination by County: 
 
A. Suspension.  County may, without cause, order Consultant in writing to 
suspend, delay or interrupt its performance in whole or part.  An adjustment 
shall be made to the completion date and for any increase in the cost of 
performance resulting from the suspension, delay or interruption.  No 
adjustment shall be made to the extent that performance is or would have been 
suspended, delayed or interrupted by another cause for which Consultant is 
responsible. 
 
B. Termination for Cause.  County may terminate this Agreement for cause if 
Consultant refuses or fails to supply enough properly skilled workers to 
perform this Agreement; fails to make required payments to subcontractors; 
disregards laws, ordinances, rules, regulations or orders of a public authority 
having jurisdiction; or otherwise commits a material breach of this Agreement. 
 When any of the foregoing causes exist, County shall give Consultant written 
notice and a ten-day opportunity to cure the default.  If the default remains 
uncured, County may (without prejudice to any other rights or remedies it may 
have) terminate this Agreement and finish the work or cause it to be finished.  
If the unpaid balance of the Agreement exceeds the cost of finishing the work 
(including any expenses incurred by County as a result of Consultant’s

Basis of Scope of Work, Revision No. 1 
 
Clarifications to Dictionary of Standardized Work Tasks: 
 
110 Location 
 
The project is located in ADOT’s Northeast District in rural Navajo County on Shumway Road 
approximately 2000 feet east of SR 77. Shumway Road is approximately four miles south of the town 
of Taylor; its alignment is generally from west to east. Project limits include the bridge, approximately 
400-500 feet of Shumway Road east and west of the bridge, and approximately 200 feet of Silver 
Creek north and south of the bridge.  
 
120 Project Description: 
 
The purpose of this project is to develop the final design for the bridge replacement on Shumway Road 
over Silver Creek in Navajo County, Arizona.  The existing bridge will be removed and replaced with 
an ADOT standard ten-cell 10’ x 8’ reinforced concrete box culvert.  Approximately 1000 feet of 
Shumway Road will be realigned horizontally and vertically. Silver Creek will be graded to deepen and 
widen the stream in the vicinity of Shumway Road. 
 
The design team will coordinate with ADOT PM, Navajo County, and ADOT’s Northeast District. The 
project is owned by Navajo County.  ADOT will administer the design and construction. 
 
This ROW scope is separate from the design scope prepared for ADOT.  The right-of-way for the 
project is locally funded by Navajo County.  This cost proposal includes: 
 
 Prepare title work, appraisals, offers, negotiations, and closings (by subconsultant Tierra).   
 Prepare the ROW plans and legals and exhibits for the acquisitions and easements (by 
subconsultant TRACE). 
 Identify ROW and easement needs for the project (Stanley Consultants). 
 Coordination with Navajo County and ADOT ROW, who is overseeing the ROW process. 
 
472 Right-of-Way Acquisition 
 
Based on the engineering design, Stanley Consultants will identify the limits for new right-of-way and 
easements.  
 
Our subconsultant, Tierra Right-of-Way, will order title reports, prepare and review appraisals, prepare 
purchase agreements and conveyance documents, negotiate with property owners on Navajo 
County’s behalf, and maintain detailed notes and contract files.  See attached cost proposal from 
Tierra. 
 
Our subconsultant, TRACE Consulting, will prepare right-of-way plans and legal descriptions/ exhibits.  
See attached cost proposal from TRACE.

 Temporary construction easements:  4 
 
The project’s design schedule is approximately twelve months. 
 
Existing ROW encroachments will not be resolved with this project.  For example, a house and a
